rows a collation or SQLite's comparison ties came in storage order, so an unchanged database could re-import as other rows and not `NO_CHANGE` (D306); a `VIRTUAL` column runs the file's expression as it is read, which D306 refuses in a DuckDB file (`trusted_schema` off limits what SQLite's can call, but the rule is the same); a table's name comes from the catalogue, not the driver's message, which can hold the URL; a noncharacter in a view's or a schema's name made the import fail with `INTERNAL_ERROR`, and 50,000 views made 50,000 notes and took 105 s; capping the notes alone still sent every name through the pipe, and `pragma_table_list` computes every view's columns in time that grows faster than their number (32,000 views, 37 s); a partition whose parent is not read vanished without a note, the immediate parent's schema misjudged a sub-partition, and a foreign partition's rows were read through the foreign server, outside the snapshot's transaction and from a database the provenance does not name; a comment in a virtual table's `sql` hid it from a match of its text, and a `rootpage` forged non-zero, which `writable_schema` can write as it can the `sql`, had a virtual table and its shadow tables read through the module; a partition pending detach, whose rows its table no longer holds, vanished without a note; a table's row security policies are its owner's expressions, run as the reader and choosing its rows (round 7's second blocker), which skipping the table avoids rather than trusting whatever the policy returns; a schema owner's `CAST` to `varchar` with a function ran as the import's role when the scanner serialised a user type, another way owner code ran on a read (round 8's first major); skipping such a table missed a cast added mid-snapshot, since cast lookup ignores the snapshot (round 9's follow-up), so every non-`pg_catalog` column is now read through its output function and resolves no cast, and only a user base type, which that function would still run, is skipped; a role's `DateStyle`, `TimeZone` or `IntervalStyle` changed the text of a `daterange` or `interval` so an unchanged database re-imported as changed (round 8's second major); a column renamed mid-snapshot mislabelled the rows read after it; a `timetz` read through Arrow lost its offset; `numeric[]` was refused as a list of doubles; a role's `quote_all_identifiers` changed a `regclass`'s text, a multirange's range was not followed, a `timetz[]` refused the import, an ordinary table's lock took its inheritance children's too, and a worker stopped while its session waited for a lock left the session waiting, holding the locks it had (round 9) |
| D310 | Connection files and URLs (M1; amends D305) | A SQLite or DuckDB connection's file must be the server's user's or root's, and neither its group nor others can write it, in a directory that the server's user or root owns and that neither its group nor others can write, unless it has the sticky bit (`PATH_NOT_CONFINED`, naming the file or the directory by its location in its import directory, never by its absolute path); the links on the way to it and the directories above it are not walked as D253 walks the configuration's, since the file's device and inode are checked when the snapshot opens it and when it ends and, where `/proc/self/fd` lists them, the files the reader opened are checked too (D306), which leaves a window only where there is no `/proc`. A Postgres or MySQL connection's URL is never given to a driver. The server reads it by one grammar of its own (`importers.urls`), `<scheme>://<user>[:<password>]@<host>[:<port>]/<database>[?<name>=<value>&…]`: the scheme `postgresql` or `postgres`, or `mysql`, in lower case; printable ASCII without a space or a `#`; exactly one `@` in the authority, and a user; one host, a DNS name, an IPv4 address in dotted decimal (a host whose last label is all digits or begins with `0x` must be one, since `getaddrinfo` reads `0177.0.0.1` in octal, `0x7f.0.0.1` in hexadecimal and `127.1` or `2130706433` in short), or a bracketed IPv6 address, without a `,` or a percent-escape, recorded in lower case or as its address's canonical text; a port from 1 to 65,535 if any; one path segment, the database, without a `/` or a control character (C0, DEL or C1, as the configuration's `schema`) once decoded, and no longer than the server keeps a name (Postgres: 63 bytes of UTF-8, and the user too; MySQL: 64 characters), a MySQL database holding only what DuckDB's scanner quotes safely (D308); the user, the password, the database and each value percent-decoded as UTF-8 without a NUL, a `+` being itself; and each parameter's name one the kind allows, once (Postgres: `application_name`, `connect_timeout`, `sslmode`, `sslrootcert`, `sslcert`, `sslkey`, `sslcrl`, `channel_binding`, `gssencmode`, `keepalives`, `keepalives_idle`, `keepalives_interval`, `keepalives_count`, `tcp_user_timeout`; MySQL: `socket`, `ssl_mode`, `ssl_ca`, `ssl_capath`, `ssl_cert`, `ssl_cipher`, `ssl_crl`, `ssl_crlpath`, `ssl_key`). Anything else is `INVALID_VALUE`, naming the connection, the variable and why, a parameter by its name when it is plainly one, never a value. The driver is given the `keyword=value` connection string built from exactly what was read (`host`, `port`, the database, `user`, `password`, the parameters), a Postgres one with `options` too (`-c search_path=pg_catalog,pg_temp -c row_security=off` and a `-c` for each output setting D309 fixes, `quote_all_identifiers=off` among them), which a startup packet sets over whatever the role or the database sets, so that the session resolves names in `pg_catalog` alone and a read that a row security policy would filter fails, each value quoted and escaped as it reads quotes (libpq's `'…'`, the MySQL scanner's `"…"`), and the provenance records the same host and database. The snapshot then reads back the database its session opened, Postgres's `current_database()` in the snapshot's transaction and MySQL's `DATABASE()` on the one pooled connection before it starts (D308), and refuses one other than the URL's as `INVALID_VALUE`, naming the server's name for it; the provenance records the database as the server named it. A MySQL URL whose host is `localhost` must give `socket`, since the scanner's client otherwise connects to its compiled-in default socket (`/tmp/mysql.sock`), which any local user can plant, and a `?` in any MySQL value (the user, the password, the database, a parameter's value) is refused, since the scanner splits the whole connection string at its first `?`, whatever the quoting; both are `INVALID_VALUE`. MySQL's `socket` names a local socket, which the client uses in place of TCP when the host is `localhost`, and which the provenance does not record, so that with it the provenance's host is `localhost` whatever server listens on that socket (a deviation from recording the server read, accepted since the URL is the operator's); Postgres's `sslkey` can name an OpenSSL engine's key (`engine:key-id`), which loads that engine; both are the operator's, as the URL is. The worker's environment holds no variable a driver reads as a default (`PGHOSTADDR`, `PGSERVICE`, `MYSQL_HOST`…, D225's worker is given `PATH`, `PYTHONPATH` and a locale alone) | Three rounds found a URL that the server's parser and libpq's read differently (query parameters that override the host, a `#` that hid them, a second `@` that made the password's text a host list or a socket path), each time with provenance naming a host other than the one read; checking for differences one at a time leaves the next, while a connection built from what the server read reaches the host it names (but for a `socket` the operator gives, which `localhost` now requires, since without it the MySQL client read a planted default socket, round 7's major); a Postgres session resolved the names its catalogue statements called through the reading role's and the database's `search_path`, which whoever could write the imported schema could use to change what they returned (round 7's first blocker), and a startup option is the one setting neither can override; Postgres cuts a database's or a user's name longer than 63 bytes short and opens the database the shorter name names, and a control character reached `source.location`, so the grammar bounds names as the servers keep them and the snapshot checks the database back rather than assume it; `getaddrinfo` read numeric shorthand as an address other than the one the provenance showed; a persistent secret beneath the worker's working directory, which is DuckDB's default with no `HOME`, gave a URL without a port or a password the ones it left out; a file others can write can be rewritten in place, which its device and inode do not show; a directory or a file another user owns lets that user swap the file; an absolute path tells a client how the server is laid out |
| D311 | The read-only catalogue page (M1; refines D255 and D265; deviates from §12.4's Frontend row, which it amends) | The server renders the catalogue page itself, as HTML without a script, a form or a remote resource, at `GET` alone: `GET /` lists the latest published release of every dataset, 50 at a time from `?offset=`, with its label, name, description, domain tags, data-use terms, packs, concepts and pack facets and its tables with their grains, roles and row counts; `GET /datasets/<id>` describes the latest published release of one dataset, 500 columns at a time from `?columns_offset=` in at most 50 tables (the next page starts at the columns of the first table not shown), with its release, effective disclosure setting, standing caveats and dataset descriptor on every page, and, for the tables the page shows, their part of the table graph (the tables, and each relationship whose child they are, with its child and parent columns and cardinality, as a table), each table's descriptor, row count and brief columns, and the relationship and coverage descriptors whose child they are and the endpoint descriptors on them (those naming no table, and coverage of an unknown relationship, on the first page), every member with its curation status and `by` (A5), so that a page holds at most 50 tables, 500 columns and what starts from them (about 200 KB, rendered in about 30 ms here, for a dataset of 1,000 tables); a Previous link never starts past the last page. Each page runs its tool (`search_catalog`, `describe_dataset`) through the server's tool calls (`Calls.tool`), as the client that asked: the same service function, disclosure settings, places, `tool_seconds` and refusals as MCP and `POST /api/tools/<name>`, the tool's refusals shown without their paths (which point into a request the browser never sent), and each request at a page path counts against the client's `page` rate (D314), never its `api` rate; the page is rendered in the call's worker thread while it holds its place, never on the event loop, so a page view holds one of the client's two tool places (D278) for as long as its tool and the rendering take (tens of milliseconds for a small dataset; for a dataset of 1,000 tables, the half-second `describe_dataset` takes to describe it, as over MCP, and the page's 30 ms), and a burst of views within the `page` rate can make an agent's call of the same client wait for its place, for seconds at the largest datasets, but never refuse it sooner than `tool_seconds`; a hit's name and description are cut as a descriptor member's value is (D312); every count is shown with its `stat:` reference, a suppressed one as suppressed and one not estimable for another reason with its reasons. Every answer at a page path (`/`, `/datasets` and below, and `/favicon.ico`, which a browser asks for) is a page, refusals included: the page's own and its tool's, request protection's (`HOST_NOT_ALLOWED`, `ORIGIN_NOT_ALLOWED`, a rate), routing's (`NOT_FOUND`, and `METHOD_NOT_ALLOWED` for `HEAD` or `POST`, with `Allow: GET`) and `INTERNAL_ERROR`, each with D265's status, refusals and blanking, as HTML (refining D265's one error shape), `Retry-After` with a 429 and a 503; every other path keeps D265's JSON. A query parameter the page does not take, one given twice, or an offset that is not at most 16 digits and at most 2^53 − 1 is `INVALID_VALUE`, naming the parameter and never echoing what was given, and so is an address whose dataset is not a dataset id, in plain words. Drafts, other labels, column descriptors (`describe_column`), the curation queue and search filters are not shown; a mount at or below `/datasets` is refused as one under `/api` is (refining D255). The React UI replaces the page in M5 | §15 ships a thin read-only page with M1 so there is something to show without an agent; rendering tool outputs on the server keeps one set of functions behind every surface (§1.1 goal 7, §11.1), so the page can show nothing an agent is not shown, suppressed counts included; a page without a script runs nothing that injected markup could reach, and needs neither a build toolchain nor generated types before M5; a person who follows a link, or mistypes an address at the page's paths, should get a page rather than JSON (an address elsewhere, such as `/index.html`, keeps D265's JSON) |
| D312 | Text on the catalogue page (M1) | Every text of the page is written as an HTML text node or attribute value, never as markup: its unsafe characters, the control characters (C0 but tab and line feed, DEL, C1), the format characters (Unicode's `Cf`, the bidi formatting, zero-width spaces, the byte order mark, soft hyphens and tag characters among them, but the zero-width non-joiner and joiner that scripts and emoji need), lone surrogates (`Cs`, which UTF-8 cannot carry) and the line and paragraph separators, become visible `\xNN`, `\uNNNN` or `\UNNNNNNNN`, each run of them in an element's text in one `<span class="escape">`, styled apart, so that it is told from the same characters in the data (a carriage return before a line feed is dropped); then `&`, `<`, `>`, `"` and `'` become character references; text from data (labels, definitions, descriptor members, pointers, statistic references, data tokens) is each in a `<bdi>` of its own. Elements and attributes come from an allow-list without scripts, embeds, forms, event handlers, `style` or `src`; a link is `/`, or segments after each `/` of letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `~`, `-` and percent-escapes, with an optional trailing `/` and a query of those characters, `=` and `&`, or a fragment of one such segment (so no tab, line feed, backslash or second `/` can turn it into a link off the server), built from the server's root path, percent-encoded, a dataset id the tool validated, a table id as a fragment, and numbers alone; a root path that is not plain segments (with `.`, `..`, an empty segment or a trailing `/`) is refused loudly, the request failing with `ValueError`, rather than dropped, since links built without it would leave the application; the stylesheet is a literal of the server's code. A descriptor member's value is its text, or its compact JSON, shown up to 2,000 characters, the rest cut and marked, and so is every other text from data (labels, names, descriptions, grains, tags and terms); a list from data (tags, facet values) shows its first 64 members and counts the rest. The operator CLI's `escaped` keeps its own narrower set (control and bidi characters) | A6 and §14: labels, definitions and cell values are rendered as plain text; escaping at the one place text is written, and building only from allowed names, makes that structural, so no rendering path can forget it; an override or an isolate in a label could otherwise reorder the text around it, invisible characters make two values that look the same differ, and an escape sequence could reach a terminal a page is copied into; a marked escape cannot pass for data that spells one, and a span per run, not per character, keeps a text of invisible characters from growing the page more than a few times; `aibi-server` sets no root path, so only an embedder can meet the refusal, and a wrong link is worse than a failure it sees |
| D313 | The catalogue page's policy (M1) | Every response at a page path (D311), refusals included, carries `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'sha256-<its stylesheet>'; img-src 'self'; connect-src 'self'; form-action 'none'; base-uri 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'`, `Cache-Control: no-store` and `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` (D314), besides request protection's headers (D255); the page's one stylesheet is inline, allowed by its hash, and loads nothing | §14's policy for the UI allows images and connections to the server only; with no script source nothing runs, and a hash admits the page's own stylesheet alone, so injected markup could neither style nor load anything; `no-store`, as the operator router's (D255), keeps a withdrawn or erased release's descriptors out of the browser's cache and history, where `no-cache` would keep them, and the page has no validators that `no-cache` could save a transfer with |
| D314 | Links from other sites to the catalogue page (M1; refines D257 and D259) | Request protection lets a top-level navigation to a page with the person's activation through its fetch-metadata check: `GET` of `/` or `/datasets/<id>` (one path segment, any query) with `Sec-Fetch-Site` `same-site` or `cross-site`, `Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate`, `Sec-Fetch-Dest: document` and `Sec-Fetch-User: ?1`, each given exactly once and exactly as a browser writes it (a header given twice, contradicting or not, or in other case, lets nothing through); an `Origin`, if given, is checked as D257 checks it. Every request at a page path, a navigation and a CORS preflight included, is counted against the client's own `page` rate (`[server.rates] page`, 120 a minute, bursts of 30; `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` naming `page_requests`, 429), never against its `api` rate. Its Host is checked first, and any other request from another site (a navigation without the person's activation, which has no `Sec-Fetch-User`; a page's subresources, frames and `fetch`; other methods, `HEAD` included; other paths, `/favicon.ico` and `/datasets/<id>/…` included; a WebSocket) is still `ORIGIN_NOT_ALLOWED` before any rate counts it. Every page response carries `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` (D313) | A lab wiki, a chat or a README links to the catalogue, and a browser that follows the link sends `Sec-Fetch-Site: cross-site`, which D257 refused, so only typed addresses and bookmarks opened it. Letting such a navigation through is safe because the page is read-only: it serves only what the public tools already show anyone who can reach the server, `GET` changes no state and runs no operator operation, the page carries no token, handle or CSRF key and reads no cookie, and the answer goes to the person's tab, which the linking site cannot read (no CORS, `frame-ancestors 'none'`). `Sec-Fetch-User: ?1` does not make a hostile page harmless: a browser sends it for a navigation the person started, and also for one a script starts while the page still has the transient activation of the person's last click or key (about 5 s in Chromium, 1 s in Firefox; assigning a window's location does not use it up), so a page the person clicks on can send a burst of navigations, to a window it opened or to its own before the first answer arrives; `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` cuts the opener's handle only once the first answer commits, and is defence in depth (`127.0.0.1` and `localhost` are secure contexts, where browsers apply it over plain HTTP; any other address needs TLS, §14). What `?1` does is refuse, before any rate, the navigations a page starts without the person having clicked on it lately. What bounds the rest is the `page` bucket: a burst of at most 30 and then two requests a second (one page view a second with the favicon a browser asks for beside it, which `no-store` lets it ask for each time), which can spend only the person's own page views and the load D311 states, and never the `api` bucket the same loopback client's agents use, so page views never make an agent's request `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` on a rate, and the tool places they hold can only make an agent's call wait (D311). Subresources stay refused before the rate is counted, as D255 has it; a browser without fetch metadata could always spend a client's rates, as on any path |
| D315 | M3 in slices (M3) | M3 is delivered in five slices, each a PR with its tests: M3.1 the registry and applicability, phase 2 of canonicalisation, `list_analyses` and `run_analysis`, `compare.existence` with its methods held to R, the disclosure of the counts a result adds, and its chart; each slice is a sub-issue of #18 (#40 to #44); M3.2 aggregates (§9.2) in the reference evaluator and the compiler, the materialised inputs a `requires` names, `summary.distribution`, `summary.members`, `compare.columns` with its bootstrap, and packs' analyses run; M3.3 `survival.km` and `survival.cox`; M3.4 the thread-count determinism tests over a fixture of a million rows (§9.3); M3.5 the result cache (§8.1). §15's exit criteria hold for M3 as a whole | `compare.existence` needs no aggregate and no endpoint, only each unit's truth value, which M2's queries already give, so it exercises the registry, phase 2, `run_analysis`, estimability and result disclosure end to end before the aggregates they will carry |
| D316 | The registry and applicability (M3.1; refines D275 and D279) | `analyses.registry.Analyses` holds the core's entries (each an analysis descriptor whose `params` and `returns` are generated from its models) and each installed pack's, by id; a view reaches an analysis only through it. `applicable(descriptors, dataset, manifest, unit?)` matches each entry's `requires`: a requirement with a `kind` is met by at least `min` (1 by default) descriptors of that kind (endpoints; columns of its `datatype`; tables other than the unit), on the unit table with `"on": "unit"`; a `predicate` requirement also needs its pack's predicate to hold of the release, one not installed, not registered or raising meeting nothing; requirements without a `kind` are the view's. `unavailable` names the roles not met (`missing`), `available_with_caveats` those met, but not by `min` descriptors without a field `imported_default`, `proposed` or `undeclared` (`unconfirmed`); without a unit, each keyed table is tried and the best status kept, each requirement predicate run once, since it reads the release and not the unit. `describe_dataset` gives it for the release it describes; `list_analyses` (`{"dataset"?, "release"?, "unit"?}`, `release` and `unit` needing `dataset`) gives every entry and, for a dataset, its applicability (`UNKNOWN_TABLE` at `/unit`); `aibi://analysis/<id>@<version>` resolves, and the resource list names every entry. A pack's analysis is registered, listed and matched from M3.1 but run from M3.2 (`NOT_SUPPORTED` at `/views/<i>/analysis`) | §9.1 and §9.4; running a pack's analysis needs the columns, aggregates and endpoint rows its `requires` names materialised (§10.1), which is M3.2's; trying each keyed table lets `describe_dataset` answer without a unit |
| D317 | Phase 2 (M3.1; revises D284, refines D299) | A view is checked in two steps around phase 1. Before it: its `analysis` must be in the registry (`UNKNOWN_ANALYSIS` at `/views/<i>/analysis`, listing its ids); its `params`, after substitution, are validated by the analysis's model, each refusal where it is written (or at the `"$name"` that supplied it); an analysis with `uses_reference` needs `cohorts` listed (`MISSING_MEMBER`); and a predicate of its parameters may hold no `ids` or `cohort` leaf at any depth (`LEAF_NOT_ALLOWED`) and at most a cohort's pack leaves. Each predicate is handed to canonicalisation as a `ViewPredicate` and resolved with the cohorts, in the view's cohorts' release, on the unit table, as a cohort of one clause (its pack leaves expanded, its caveat rules run, its refusals below `/views/<i>/params/predicates/<j>`). After it, a view whose cohorts and predicates all canonicalised gets its canonical form: the analysis id and version, cohort ids in view order (by computation id when it lists none, D284), `reference` as a position (the first by default) for `uses_reference`, `overlap` for `assumes_independent_groups`, and its canonical parameters, every default written and each predicate its canonical clause tree; its effective *k* is the largest of its cohorts', its predicates' and the floor; its packs are those of its analysis, cohorts and predicates. `validate_document` and `count_cohort` return each view that checked (`position`, `analysis`, ids, the log's status of its result id, form, readback, the caveats that need no data), and a view refused refuses `count_cohort` too, but for one of a core analysis of §9.5 that a later slice implements (`summary.distribution`, `summary.members` and `compare.columns` in M3.2, `survival.km` and `survival.cox` in M3.3): that is `NOT_SUPPORTED` at `/views/<i>/analysis`, naming the slice, which `validate_document` lists among its refusals and `run_analysis` refuses, while `count_cohort` counts the cohorts beside it. A view whose cohorts are of more than one release is `MIXED_RELEASES` at `/views/<i>/cohorts`, which resolution refuses first. A view's readback names its cohorts by position and states each predicate from the release's descriptors | §7.4 and §7.6; a predicate asked of every unit is not a set of units, so `ids` and `cohort` leaves belong in a cohort; resolving predicates as cohorts of one clause reuses phase 1 whole, so a predicate's leaf keys, caveats and SQL are a cohort's |
| D318 | `run_analysis` (M3.1; refines D300) | Takes `{"document"}`, loads and checks it as `count_cohort` does, and fails with the first refusal; a document without views is `MISSING_MEMBER` at `/views`. Each cohort the views name is counted once by computation id, and each view's crossing of its cohorts with its predicates (each predicate that holds a `lift` under the other lift rule too) once, views asking the same crossing sharing it, in one query worker (`run_crossed`), which ends `RECORD_SECONDS` before the deadline. A crossing is integer `COUNT(*)`s in DuckDB, one statement per cohort that joins its units once with every predicate and groups them by `GROUPING SETS`: per cohort and predicate, the units by truth code and reasons, with those the other lift rule changes and the flags' `BIT_OR`; per cohort, its units for which some predicate is known, and those for which none is by reason; and the units each pair of cohorts shares. No query gives a row per unit, so an answer's size depends on the number of cohorts and predicates, never on the units, and the answer cap bounds no view (an answer over it would name the widest view, `/views/<i>`); the work does grow with the units: over a million units and 16 predicates, 3 cohorts took 10 s on one thread and 5 s on four, and 6 cohorts 22 s and 8 s, within the default `query_memory` of 2 GiB (a branch per predicate and a join of all of them, as first written, took 11 s, 5 s, 23 s and ran out of memory on four threads), so 6 cohorts at that scale on one thread come near the default `query_seconds` of 25 and want more threads or seconds (on predicates of the unit's columns and one step down; D373 gives M3.4's figures on related rows, where the same over 6 cohorts took 71 s on one thread, past `query_seconds`, and 16 predicates over 3 cohorts need 4 GiB). The SQL compiler must give the reference evaluator's result, digest included, and a crossing what `cross` counts from each unit's truth value. A view of an analysis with `assumes_independent_groups` whose cohorts share units, without `overlap: "allow"`, refuses the call (`COHORTS_OVERLAP` at `/views/<i>`), its `counts` the count of each pair's shared units (the canonical intersection of the two cohorts), counted in a second worker run, disclosed and issued as `count_cohort`'s; a shared part whose form would pass a cohort's cap on leaves (§7.1), which two cohorts within it can (its depth is its deeper cohort's), is not counted, and refuses the call (`LIMIT_EXCEEDED` at `/views/<i>`, naming the cap). Each view's result is recorded as one issuance, naming the call's request and `{"queries": [{"statements", "parameters"}, …]}` of its cohorts' counts and its crossing as run, and each cohort the views name as one of its own, so `explain` resolves every id a result names, for as long as the result is kept (a count issuance recorded with a kept result's request, over its release, is kept with it, and results are pruned before counts); all in one transaction under D300's rules (deadline, withdrawal, erasure mark, `log_bytes`). It returns `{results, params}`, one envelope per view in document order; each envelope's derivation `document` holds the canonical view and each of its cohorts' hashed objects, and its `source` the leaves of the view's cohorts and predicates as written. It is not read-only. Results are cached from M3.5 (D376): a hit's issuance has no SQL and names as `values_from` the issuance whose queries gave its values, and the cohorts only hit views name are not counted, their ids resolved through the source's call's counts. An issuance of a count, whichever tool issued it (its cohorts', and a refused call's shared parts), expires with `count_cohort`'s after `keep_count_issuances_days`; an issuance of a result after `[log] keep_result_issuances_days` (365 by default, 0 keeping them until an operator prunes, at most 36,500); `aibi prune --before` prunes every issuance before the time, and without it each kind before its period, as the log's thread does, refused only when both are kept (`Pruned` names both times, each absent when that kind is kept); migration 6 lets pruning's permit remove any issuance recorded before its time that no kept issuance recorded later names (an issuance names as `values_from` only one of its own derivation, so of its own kind) | §8.1, §11.1 and §12.2; one worker run bounds a document's queries together (§14); an overlap refused with the size of what the cohorts share tells the client how to fix it (§7.4); issuances that never expire filled `log_bytes` for good after a few thousand calls of large documents, refused ones included, with no operator remedy; a result is what a finding cites, so it is kept longer than a count, and v1 promises no availability of an id (§7.6), whose document run again gives it and its digest again; each unit's truth value for every query filled the 64 MiB answer cap at about a dozen queries over a million units, where §9.3 promises that scale |
| D319 | `compare.existence` (M3.1) | Version 1.0.0; parameters `predicates` (1 to 16 clauses) and `level` (0.95; at most 1 − 10⁻⁹, `INVALID_VALUE` above, since the normal quantile of a level within 2⁻⁵³ of 1 is infinite). Per position and predicate: the proportion TRUE over units for which the predicate is known (Wilson), and `lift_differs`; `analysed` counts the units for which some predicate is known, `excluded` every reason any predicate gave the rest, and with two or more predicates `variables` gives each predicate's. Per predicate: the test over the cohorts with known units (Fisher's for two, Pearson's chi-squared without continuity correction for more, `SMALL_N` when an expected count is below 5), the risk difference (Newcombe hybrid score) and ratio (Katz log) of every other position versus the reference; Benjamini–Hochberg over the tests computed (`family`: `tests`, `not_computed`, `suppressed`). Estimability, first reason first: shared units under `overlap: "allow"` (`overlapping_cohorts`, no between-cohort value, `COHORTS_OVERLAP`); then, under *k*, suppression; `no_units`; `degenerate_table`; `no_events` for a zero numerator of a ratio. | D320 | Disclosure of results (M3.1; refines §8.4) | A result's population is disclosed position by position as a cohort count is (D297), so a cohort's counts are the same in a result as in `count_cohort`'s answer. At each position, each predicate's split of the cohort's units (TRUE, FALSE and UNKNOWN) is disclosed as a cohort count is, by `engine.suppression.suppressed`, with the cohort's `n_true` as its size (`analyses.disclosure`): a proportion shows its numerator where the TRUE units are shown, its denominator and the predicate's `excluded_units` where the UNKNOWN units are; a position whose `n_true` is suppressed shows none of its predicates' counts; `analysed` of one predicate is its split's, and of more shows nothing that combines predicates (`n`, `excluded_units` and `excluded` `null`; each predicate's `variables` entry shown as its split is): the predicates' shown counts bound a joint count from both sides (at least Σu − (m − 1)·N units with no predicate known, at most the fewest u) and a union of reasons bounds a predicate's own map, so a combined figure may be shown only where the counts shown give it alone. the first rule linked a numerator only to its denominator, so a numerator of 9 shown beside a suppressed denominator in a cohort of 10 gave away its one unit not TRUE, and that it was UNKNOWN; a split disclosed as a cohort count has D297's symmetry, so `not P` is disclosed as `P` with TRUE and FALSE swapped and the same count under two names is suppressed in both; | D321 | Statistical methods and the R reference (M3.1) | `analyses.stats` computes each method from counts in doubles, in a fixed order, sums by `math.fsum`, no DuckDB aggregate: the normal quantile as `statistics.NormalDist` (AS 241), Wilson as `prop.test(correct = FALSE)`, Newcombe's method 10, Katz, Fisher's two-sided test as `fisher.test` (tables no likelier than observed to a relative 1e-7, by the ratio of consecutive hypergeometric probabilities from the mode), Pearson's chi-squared as `chisq.test(correct = FALSE)` with the regularised upper gamma (series, or a Lentz continued fraction), BH as `p.adjust`. Fisher's probabilities, relative to the mode's, are held as a mantissa and a binary exponent (`math.frexp`), which scaling by powers of two leaves exact, so they give a double's bits wherever it is normal and a p-value is 0 only below the least double (`[[1887, 1414], [2946, 152]]` gives R's 4.81e-306, where a double tail cut at 1e-300 gave 0). A question: TRUE or FALSE, UNKNOWN excluded under its reasons. An aggregate pools (`Evaluator.pool`): at each step the ups are followed (`NO_PARENT`), a parent outside the parent scope is `OUT_OF_SCOPE`, and one not closed for the values the last step's conditions admit on the scope columns adds its closedness reasons (`COVERAGE_PROPOSED` for a parent in scope over proposed coverage, `SCOPE_PARTIAL` for one closed for listed tuples); at an earlier step each child is pooled through and dropped when unknown only for reasons its lift drops, none kept adding `NOT_COVERED` and a kept unknown child its reasons; at the last step a row whose conditions and record filter are TRUE is pooled, UNKNOWN adds their reasons. A unit with a reason is excluded under all of them; otherwise `count` counts its rows, and `max`, `min` and `mean` read each row's value through the lookups, skip NOT_APPLICABLE, and are excluded `NO_INFORMATION`, `NOT_ASSESSED` or `NO_PARENT` for the rest, an ordered category compared by listed position (a value outside the list `NO_INFORMATION`), `empty` or `NO_ROWS` with no value; `mean` pools and gives the exact mean correctly rounded (`exact_mean`: each value, integer or double, a ratio of integers over a power of two, their exact sum over the largest, divided once by Python's correctly rounded division of integers, which cannot overflow; `math.fsum` divided once would round twice, an ulp off in about one sample in eight); doubles are normalised (`-0.0` is `0.0`). Variables compute no `lift_differs`. The compiler (`sql.compile_materialised`) gives one `UNION ALL` statement per cohort and variable, whose rows are tagged: units per value, units excluded per set of reasons (their bits in `ExclusionReason` order) with the flags' `BIT_OR`, units taking `empty`, and for `max` and `min` the units of each unit's greatest or least value, which DuckDB picks (`MAX`, `MIN`: exact, so allowed over doubles, §9.3), and for `mean` each unit's pooled rows grouped by value, which the server aggregates; with two variables or more, one statement per cohort counting its units by whether some variable has a value and, for none, the bits of all their reasons. Every count is an integer `COUNT` and values are grouped, never summed or averaged, by DuckDB (§9.3). A Hypothesis property holds the compiler to the evaluator on every variable kind over random cities, and a second one on multi-valued categories alone, ordered ones under `max` and `min` among them, over rows NOT_APPLICABLE, NOT_ASSESSED, outside the list or null (an ordered category's NOT_APPLICABLE row is skipped in SQL as in the evaluator, not NO_INFORMATION) | §9.2's pooling, made executable as §13.3 asks, with every rule the evaluator applies checked against SQL; averaging in DuckDB would put a DOUBLE aggregate into a digest, which D294 forbids |

Under *k* such a refusal depends on the data, for a materialisation on how many distinct values its cohorts' units hold, and is not disclosed by the count rule (§8.4 Limits). The server reads a materialisation's answer, for `mean` a list per unit, outside `query_memory`, bounded by the answer cap, and stops at the call's deadline (`CallerDeadline`, looked at every 65,536 rows, and every 65,536 units while a `mean`'s units are averaged), and `summarise` looks at it before each column, so a call overruns it by one column's statistics at most (about 4 s over a million distinct values); M3.4 measured it at a million units (D373: a `mean` over a million units, 10.2 s on one thread and 8.6 s on four, the server at 430 MB); at 1,000,000 units with 1,500,000 rows, a `mean` of doubles gives 1.5 million rows, which M3.2a read in about 7 s, and `max` and `min` give a row per distinct value | D318's answers were integer counts alone; a variable's values are doubles and text, and grouping by value in DuckDB then aggregating in Python keeps D294's rule while the answer carries only distinct values |

Over 24 units under *k* = 4, `X` of 8, 13 and 3 units shows only its 13 (D297), and the lists of every unit (24 keys), of `not X` (13) and of `X and known X` (8, a collapse, §8.4's Limits), each of whose sizes is shown, would name the 3 units whose `X` is unknown, so the differencing the Limits disclaim for counts would name the units behind every count the pass hides, in one call; D329 names no value that few units hold for the same reason |
| D333 | Keys in the evaluator and the compiler (M3.2b; refines D318 and D327) | `engine.members.keys` gives a cohort's members' keys by the reference evaluator, and `sql.compile_members` by SQL: one statement per cohort that joins its truth values to the unit table's key columns, a row per member by `rid`, each key column as stored (text and doubles as value columns, D327), a date as its days since 1970-01-01 (`date_diff`) and a datetime as its microseconds since the epoch (`epoch_us`), which the server reads back into dates and datetimes in UTC; a `NULL` or a value of another type is a fault (`QueryError`), found when the key is read. The statement gives at most `MAX_LISTED` + 1 rows (1,000,001, by `rid`): a cohort of more than `MAX_LISTED` members refuses the call (`LIMIT_EXCEEDED` at `/views/<i>`, `listed_members`). `run_analysis` lists the keys of each cohort a view of it names once, by computation id, in its one worker run (`queries.run_views`, `members`); the answer grows with the cohort (8 bytes a unit for an integer key) and the answer cap bounds it too: an answer over it, or a run over its seconds or memory, names the first view that lists keys, which reads a row per member, before the widest materialisation. The server holds the keys as the answer packs them and makes each when it is read (`sql.ListedKeys`), and takes each view's page (`members.select`) by making each member's sort key (its `order_key` with its index appended, which orders as the key alone, since no key's serialisation begins another's), sorting them in runs of 65,536 and merging the runs lazily up to the page's last key: so it holds one `bytes` per member outside `query_memory` (at the cap, on four cores: 4 to 4.5 s and about 93 MB for 64-bit integer keys, 4 s and 78 MB for 12-character text ones, 6.5 s and 107 MB for datetimes, written with `isoformat`, 8 s by `strftime`; a key of several columns costs about the sum of its parts; listings at the cap under `tool_calls` concurrency hold the server's CPU for up to the deadline each, as M3.4 measured, D373), sorts at most a run at once, and looks at the call's deadline, less `RECORD_SECONDS`, between runs and every 65,536 keys merged (`CallerDeadline`, the call refused as late). A datetime is written with a four-digit year (`resolved.constant_json`, by `isoformat`, as D289 writes the log's times): the platform's `strftime` wrote a year before 1000 without its zeros on Linux and with them elsewhere, so such a key or constant did not read back and its order and ids depended on the platform. The semantics version is not bumped: the fix changes only the canonical forms, and so the ids, of cohorts, predicates and views that hold a datetime before the year 1000, which on Linux did not canonicalise to themselves (their text was refused when read back); no id keeps its text with another digest (§7.6's invariant), and no membership, count or caveat changes. Each result's issuance records its cohort's count and then the listing's statement and parameters, which hold no key, so the derivation log keeps no key a result listed (D318) and erasure has none to find beyond the constants a document wrote (D290); the result cache (M3.5) caches no `summary.members` result, whose values are keys. Hypothesis properties hold the compiler to the evaluator over keys of every stored type (integers beyond ±(2^53 − 1), doubles, booleans, text outside the Basic Multilingual Plane, dates and datetimes from the year 1), of one column and of two, and a page's keys written into an `ids` leaf to the same members | D318's queries gave no row per unit, and a list of keys is one by nature; the answer cap bounds the worker's answer but not what the server builds from it, which review measured at 1.9 GB and 23 s for 8,000,000 integer keys within the cap, so a listing has a cap of its own and the page is taken without an object per member; ordering on the server keeps §7.6's order, which no SQL collation gives; a key kept in the log or a cache would outlive the erasure of its unit, whose redaction reads the constants documents write |

The reports go to `server/reports/`, which git ignores. For the view, per column: for categories, the test of the table of the positions with a value by the categories some of them hold (Fisher's for two by two, else chi-squared, `SMALL_N` below an expected 5) and, per row of the table, the `proportion_difference` of each other position versus the reference with Newcombe's hybrid score interval; for numbers, the primary test (`welch_t` for two positions used, `welch_anova` for more, whose second degrees of freedom are `df_denominator`), the secondary (`mann_whitney` or `kruskal_wallis`, unadjusted, no q-value) and, per other position, the `mean_difference` (Welch–Satterthwaite interval) and the `median_difference` (bootstrap percentile interval); Benjamini–Hochberg over the columns' primary tests (`family`). Estimability, first reason first: overlap (`overlapping_cohorts`), suppression, `no_units`, `degenerate_table`, `zero_variance` (a group of one value, of equal values, or whose standard deviation is below the least double: its `sd`, every Welch test that includes it and the interval, not the estimate, of a difference in means; every value tied: both rank tests); a test not computed for overlap or suppression names no position and the method the view's cohorts (and a table's rows) give. Without *k*, a number beyond ±(2^53 − 1) among the values, statistics, differences or bounds refuses the call (`NOT_SUPPORTED` at `…/columns/<j>`), as D328's does, not the column alone: the refusal names the column to drop, and a column not estimable for its size would need a reason of its own in both analyses; Welch's tests are computed so that groups of any scales a double holds are compared (D338), so only a result truly beyond the bound refuses, a statistic beyond a double's range among them. Each position's moments and median are computed once for its summary, the tests and the differences, at a cost of its distinct values, not its units; the call's deadline is checked before each column and each of its steps (a position's values in order, its moments, its median, the tests, a position's bootstrap; for categories, each position's values counted), and within each of them every 65,536 values (`stats._CHUNK`: a position's values sorted in runs of that many merged two at a time, `stats.checked_sort`; the passes of its moments, its median and its bootstrap; the rank tests' counts, ranks and ties); a column of categories whose declared values are more than `MAX_CATEGORIES` is refused before any value is read, else at the first undeclared value too many, before any is sorted, membership in the declared values is a set's and each position's units with a value are summed once. So a call overruns its deadline by one stretch at most: at the answer cap (`query_answer_bytes`, 64 MiB, D293), which holds about five million distinct values (13 bytes a row), about 1.2 s whatever its shape, a rank test's sums or the last merge of a sort (one cohort of 4.8 million, two of 2.4 million, six of 800,000, measured on a shared four-core machine, where round 4 found 8.6 s in one cohort's moments; a category column of 10,000 declared values beside two cohorts of 500,000 others is refused at once, and under *k* six cohorts of 333,000 undeclared values take 0.3 s, where round 3 found 60 s and 10 s); round 1 found 51 s past a deadline of 1 s. `analysed`, `UNKNOWN_EXCLUDED` and the readback's columns as D328's, `COHORTS_OVERLAP` under `overlap: "allow"`, the readback naming cohorts by position, and one chart per column (a category's bars as D330's; a number's differences as points with their intervals, a row only for one shown) | §9.5 named the methods and left the parameters, the shape and the estimability of the between-cohort values open; categories and numbers as `summary.distribution` takes them keep one reading of a variable across the core's analyses |
| D337 | Disclosure of comparisons (M3.2c; refines §8.4, D320 and D329; revises §8.4's linked cohort × category table) | `compare.columns` is a disclosed analysis (D353), by this rule. Under *k*, a position's population, its variables' splits and `analysed` are disclosed as `summary.distribution`'s (D329), and its categories merged by `disclosure.merged`, so that one column's rows are the same in both analyses in one call. The view's table is the coarsest grouping of the listed rows of which every shown position's merged rows are unions (a group starts only where every shown position's merged rows start one), each count the sum of rows shown; a test that needs a position whose split is not shown, and a difference either of whose positions is one, is `suppressed`. Of numbers only `n` is shown: `mean`, `sd`, `median`, both tests, both differences and their intervals are `suppressed`, whatever the counts, the tests leaving the family; so under *k* applicability says `compare.columns` is `unavailable` for a unit over which no view compares categories: its table has no column of categories (`category`, `boolean`, `list<category>`) and is in no relationship (a coverage table is in none: the release's checks refuse one), through which a path reaches every other table and down which, directly or with a `via` back down the one it went up, `some` and `every` make categories of any column; without a unit, the best status over the keyed tables; `missing` names `columns` and `min_cell_count` (`registry.CATEGORIES_UNDER_K`). `test_columns_disclosure` checks, over every way the first of two cohorts of 8 to 11 units splits over three declared categories, an undeclared value and units excluded, beside two second cohorts, under *k* from 3 to 5, that the whole output (population, analysed, both positions' rows, the table, its test, every difference and interval, the family and the caveats) names no undeclared value and shows no count from 1 to *k* − 1 and, among the outputs that read the same, leaves every count two values or more unless the first position's rows give it (the undeclared value's never pinned to one from 1 to *k* − 1); | D338 | Methods of comparisons and their bootstrap (M3.2c; refines §9.3, §9.5 and D321) | `analyses.stats` computes each position's `moments` once (n, the mean as a double and exactly, and the standard deviation as a mantissa and a binary exponent, over values scaled as D328's `sd` scales them, around the exact mean rounded less the square of its rounding, so that a spread of a few ulps is exact to rounding, a value many units hold one exact product), Welch's *t* as `t.test(var.equal = FALSE)` (the statistic the first used position's mean less the second's, the standard error by `hypot`, the Welch–Satterthwaite degrees of freedom from the errors' shares) and its interval as `conf.int`, Welch's one-way test as `oneway.test(var.equal = FALSE)`: Welch's *t* over the errors scaled by the largest group's power of two, which leaves it as it is, and the one-way test with its weights, their shares and the weighted centre as exact rationals and each group's distance from the centre standardised on its own scale, so that groups whose spreads differ by any power of two a double holds are tested (a Hypothesis property holds t², F and both degrees of freedom to 1e-12 of their exact rational values over groups scaled from 2^-1074 to 1), Mann–Whitney as `wilcox.test(correct = TRUE)` (`W` of the first group; exact below 50 values a group without ties, by the integer counts of the Gaussian binomial coefficient, else the normal approximation with its tie and continuity corrections) and Kruskal–Wallis as `kruskal.test` (ranks of the values as doubles, as `rank` gives them, and the correction for ties over the values that `as.character` prints alike, as `table` groups them: `stats._printed` is R 4.3's `formatReal` at 15 digits with its long double scaling emulated exactly and fixed notation wherever it is no wider, so 0.1 + 0.2 and 0.3 are two ranks and one tie while 1234567890123456 and 1234567890123457 are two; it is x86-64 R's, whose long double is x87's 64-bit mantissa, and an R without one prints some values otherwise, so its ties differ (arm64 macOS, where a long double is a double, about 1 in 100 values near a rounding half; aarch64 Linux, a quad, a few in 10⁵), so `columns.json` records the platform that wrote it and `test_stats` requires x86-64; where every value prints alike R's statistic is infinite, and it is not computed, `zero_variance`), both rank tests' sums integers of doubled mid-ranks and their statistics exact rationals, rounded once, with the tails of the regularised incomplete beta function and its inverse (a safeguarded Newton iteration), as `pt`, `qt` and `pf`: for a from 15, b to 40 and x above 0.7, by the asymptotic expansion of DiDonato and Morris (TOMS 708's `bgrat`), b reduced to (0, 1] by a recurrence of positive terms, and otherwise by its continued fraction by Lentz, whose first terms cancel for large a near 1; its factor x^a·(1 − x)^b / B(a, b) by Stirling's series for a or b from 10, `log1p` taken of a ratio less 1 only below ½ (TOMS 708's `rlog1`). `tests/core/analyses/reference/columns.R` (R 4.3.3) wrote `columns.json`, and every value agrees to 1e-10 relative (a Welch statistic within 1e-12 of 0, where R's two-pass mean cancels), degrees of freedom to 1e8 and `pbeta`'s parameters to 8e6 included; over 16,000 further points against R, t and F tails agree to 3e-13 and t quantiles to 1e-13, and I_x(a, b) to 3e-13 where one parameter is 40 or less (over 258 points of the expansion's region against mpmath) and 2e-10 where both exceed it near the mode, which Welch's tests never reach; levels stop at 0.999, beyond which `qt` is itself inexact (1e-7 at 1 − 1e-9, where the implementation is within 2e-14 of the exact quantile). The bootstrap resamples each cohort within itself `REPLICATES` = 2000 times, the reference's replicates shared by every difference versus it; each replicate's median is drawn from its exact law under resampling with replacement: a resample's order statistics are the values at ⌊n·U₍ⱼ₎⌋ of the sorted values, U₍ⱼ₎ ~ Beta(j, n − j + 1) as a ratio of Marsaglia–Tsang gammas (normals by `NormalDist.inv_cdf`), the next U₍ⱼ₎ + (1 − U₍ⱼ₎)·(1 − V^(1/(n − j))), so a replicate costs the same at any n. Its stream is Python's `random.Random`, whose `random()` Python keeps the same for an integer seed, seeded by the SHA-256 of the RFC 8785 text of `{"column", "computation", "position"}`, the computation id omitting the disclosure setting (D144); a uniform of 0 is drawn again. The interval takes the ⌈B·α/2⌉-th and ⌈B·(1 − α/2)⌉-th replicates, α = 1 − level read as its shortest decimal (0.95 gives the 50th and the 1950th, where the double's own α, above 0.05, would give the 51st). Determinism is of the inputs: the stream is seeded from them and no clock is read; the methods of comparisons call `log`, `log1p`, `expm1` and `lgamma`, which not every platform rounds correctly, so a platform's library can change a p-value's last bit, or flip a gamma sampler's acceptance about once in 10¹³ calls, where D321's `fisher` uses basic operations only and is the same everywhere. `HypothesisTest.df` holds a positive number for Welch's tests, and `df_denominator` an F test's second | §9.3 asks for resampling seeded from the computation id and exact algorithms, and §13.4 names R as the reference; drawing each of n units for each of 2000 replicates is 2·10⁹ draws at a million units, where the order statistics' exact law gives the same distribution with two gammas a replicate; R's generator cannot be matched, so the bootstrap is held to that law by counting over every resample of small groups and beside resampling value by value, not to R; Welch's tests are invariant under a common scale, and without it errors whose squares underflow (spreads below about 1e-154) crashed or refused; the continued fraction alone was off by up to 4e-9 at a million degrees of freedom, which Welch's reach at about 4e5 units a group, and R's `pt` there is not an approximation but agrees with Boost to 1e-15; `kruskal.test`'s correction is R's, held to it as every method here is, although grouping ties as doubles would be cleaner, and grouping at 15 significant digits is not R's (fixed notation prints every integer digit from 1e15, where 388 of 400 datasets disagreed); the one-way test's means scaled by the least spread overflowed for groups whose spreads differ by about 1e154 |

The view's cohorts number what a `cohorts` requirement allows (`INVALID_VALUE` at `/views/<i>/cohorts`); `uses_reference` needs them listed (D317). Its canonical parameters are `{"columns": {role: [variable forms]}, "options"}`, the options as written after substitution; its readback states the analysis's label and id as data, its cohorts by count and its reference by position, each column by role (D325) and the options as data; its packs, which its ids hash, are its analysis's with its cohorts', predicates' and variables' | §9.1 and §10.1 left how a view binds a `requires` entry to columns and where a pack's own settings go; roles as keys are fixed by the entry, never chosen by the user (§7.4), and a schema the pack declares checks what only the pack understands, where it is written; a pack's result depends on its version as a core analysis's does on its own |
| D342 | A pack analysis's inputs (M3.2d; refines §9.3, §10.1, D318, D327 and D333) | `AnalysisInputs` holds the analysis id and entry version, its input columns (`InputColumn`: role, column, kind, function, the values' datatype, canonical form), per position in view order the cohort's members (its units for which it is TRUE) with each column's value or `None` and the reasons it is excluded (`ExclusionReason` names, sorted), column by column (`InputPosition`), the reference's position, whether the view's cohorts share units (`overlapping`, which an analysis that assumes independent groups is handed only under `overlap: "allow"`, since it is refused otherwise), a copy of the options and a seed. Members are ordered as §9.3 orders rows, by their unit keys' RFC 8785 serialisation compared as UTF-16 code units (`inputs.ordered`, by `members.ranked`), never by DuckDB; the keys order them and are never handed: an analysis reads values, not identities. So an input column that reads an identifier column's values (§5.4: declared `identifier`, a table's primary key, or a relationship's column on either side, the one rule `resolve.identifying` that resolution applies too), by any aggregate but `count`, which reads none, is refused (`ROW_IDS_NOT_ALLOWED` at `…/column`, naming `count`), and so is one whose `where` tests an identifier column, by a value leaf or a `covered` leaf's scope, which would hand a value computed from it (`ROW_IDS_NOT_ALLOWED` at `…/where`), whatever the dataset allows; and since values in the keys' order are a table of units a pack can give back, no view is run where the dataset allows no row ids (D344). `engine.inputs.listed` gives them by the reference evaluator (`members.keys` and `variables.evaluate_variable`), and `sql.compile_inputs` by SQL: the members of all its cohorts taken once, each cohort's first `MAX_LISTED` + 1 by `rid` and of them the first `most` + 1 by position and `rid`, `most` being `MAX_INPUT_CELLS` over the view's columns, so that no statement ships more members' rows than the caps admit over the view's positions together; per cohort, one statement that lists them with their keys (a cohort of more than 1,000,000 members refuses the call, `LIMIT_EXCEEDED` at the first view of a pack's analysis that lists it, `listed_members`, and positions of more than `most` members together, `input_cells`, both known when the server reads the listings' rows, before it reads a variable's; the worker has run every statement by then, so a view over the cap spends their time first: 3 cohorts with 2 columns over 1,330,000 members, 25.5 s of SQL, `query_seconds` before `input_cells` at the default limits) and one per variable whose rows are a materialisation's (D326) with a row per member rather than per value, `r` its `rid`: its value, its reasons' bits, `empty`, its greatest or least value as DuckDB picks it, or for `mean` its pooled rows by value, which the server averages exactly (`exact_mean`); a member with no row or two, a row for another unit, or a flag word no flag sets, is a fault (`QueryError`). Every relation the statements read is materialised once (`AS MATERIALIZED`), and a variable's statement stacks its branches from one relation, the join of the members to their units, which no branch joins again: DuckDB planned the join inside the branches of the union of a row per member so that a `mean`, `max`, `min` or `some` one or two to-many steps down did not end from about 500,000 units, inlined or materialised. A listing's time grows with the unit table's size times its cohorts times its columns, not with its cells, since each variable's relation is computed over every unit of the table in each cohort's statement (follow-up #56, for materialisations too). Measured on a 1,000,000-unit table: one cohort of all its units and one column lists in 11 to 16 s end to end at the default limits (two to-many steps down in 11 to 12 s), and in 3.6 to 5.1 s of SQL in one thread; but a cohort of 2,500 members with 8 columns takes 20.5 s of SQL, 3 cohorts with 2 columns 15.8 s, and 6 cohorts with 8 columns 130 s, over `query_seconds`, so a view within the cells cap is not always listed within `tool_seconds`. `test_variables` holds the statements' shape, and a sweep of every kind of variable (a column, and each aggregate and question one and two steps down, with and without a `where`) over 600,000 units in one thread, each statement in 1 to 2.3 s under a time limit, where the old shape did not end four of them in 30 s. Two Hypothesis properties hold the compiler to the evaluator (every variable kind over random cities, with no variable and with up to three; and multi-valued categories alone, ordered ones meeting rows not applicable, not assessed and outside the list), keys, rows, values, reasons and flags alike, once ordered. `run_analysis` lists each view's inputs once in its one worker run (`queries.run_views(…, inputs=)`, views with the same cohorts' computation ids and variables' forms sharing it), and an answer over the cap, or a run over its seconds or memory, names the first view that lists keys or inputs; the server reads the listing's rows by the call's deadline. The server holds each member's values and reasons (the reasons' sets shared) and orders them without making a key (`_Reordered`); the inputs hold at most `MAX_INPUT_CELLS` = 1,000,000 cells over the view's positions, each position's members times its columns, one at least (`LIMIT_EXCEEDED` at `/views/<i>`, `input_cells`), as many as a listing reads members; a listing shared by several views is ordered once. The seed is the SHA-256 of the RFC 8785 text of `{"computation"}`, the view's computation id, as an integer, so that a pack's resampling is reproducible as the core's is (D338). Each result's issuance records its cohorts' counts and then the listing's statements and parameters, which hold no key and no value, so the log keeps neither (D318, D333). Units two cohorts share are found from the listings' rows, for every view: a view of an analysis that assumes independent groups whose cohorts share units, without `overlap: "allow"`, refuses the call with the shared parts' counts as a materialisation's does (D339) | §10.1 names the inputs as per position the units with the columns and aggregates, materialised by the core; a row per member and variable is what a pack's per-unit methods need, and the listing of keys (D333) already bounds a row per member; an order from the keys is the one order the server defines for units, the same by SQL and by the evaluator and on every platform, where a pack handed rows in storage order would give one release's result two digests; but the order is by identity, and an identifier column's values are identities (§5.4), declared ones as much as keys: the values never reach a pack, and the order reaches none where a unit's key may not be listed |
| D343 | Running a pack's analysis (M3.2d; refines §8.1, §10.1 and D285) | `analyses.packs.run_pack` hands `run` inputs of its own and runs it once, in the server's process, as a pack's code runs everywhere (packs are installed through code review, §9.1, and trusted not to read what they are not handed); the call's deadline, less `RECORD_SECONDS`, is looked at before `run`, after it, after its values are checked and after they are counted, and throughout the check of its `returns` schema (every `TIMED_STEPS` = 4,096 steps, `jsonschemas.TimedBudget`) and the counting of `analysed` (every `DEADLINE_UNITS` = 65,536 units). A pack's code cannot be stopped, so a call overruns its deadline by what its `run` takes past it; after it, by the copy of its values, which the caps bound (0.13 s for 200,000 values), and by one stretch of the core's work between two looks: 4,096 steps of the check, one column's count, or 65,536 members of the joint count (measured with the deadline passed, at most 0.34 s for the check and 0.65 s for the counting, within `RECORD_SECONDS`). What `run` gives must be JSON that text carries unchanged (`pack_api.plain_json`: finite numbers within ±(2^53 − 1), Unicode text, no value that holds itself, no depth beyond 64), at most `MAX_RESULT_VALUES` = 200,000 values (`result_values`) and `MAX_RESULT_CHARACTERS` = 8 MiB of characters of text, keys included (`result_characters`), each string at most `MAX_TEXT` = 10,000 (`text_characters`), each counted before it is copied, so that no more is copied than a result holds (over one, `PACK_FAILED` names the limit), satisfying the entry's `returns` schema as registered within the steps of an object of that many values, shaped as a result's `values` (`{"positions": [an object per position], "view": {…}}`, nothing else) whose `not_estimable` maps name null members (`Values`); anything else, and any exception raised while `run` runs or while what it gave is read (a mapping's `items` and a sequence's iteration run the pack's code), `BaseException`'s subclasses included but `MemoryError`, `KeyboardInterrupt` and `SystemExit` themselves, which pass by identity as new instances without the pack's message, traceback or context (a pack's subclass of one, or a type that says it equals one, is its failure), refuses the call (`PACK_FAILED` at `/views/<i>/analysis`, with a message of the core's, never the exception's, and never a key or a path of what the pack gave: a `returns` failure names the schema's keyword alone; the log names the pack, the stage and a built-in exception's type, found by identity in `builtins`, and any other as the pack's own, since a pack names its classes and their `__module__`). A limit's error that the pack's own code raises while its values are copied (`JsonTooLarge`) is its failure, not a limit: the copy knows the error it raised itself. The other hooks (leaf compilers and summaries, translators, predicates, caveat rules) catch `Exception` as they did; bringing them to this rule is a follow-up, #55. The values are data (A6) and rounded for the digest as every output's are (§9.3). The result's population is each position's cohort count; `analysed` per position is each column's units with a value and excluded by reason, and with two columns or more the units some column has a value for, as `summary.distribution`'s (D328), and without columns the members, none excluded; its caveats are the cohorts' (§8.3), `UNKNOWN_EXCLUDED` naming the values where a member is excluded for a reason other than `NOT_APPLICABLE`, the flags of the values read, `COHORTS_OVERLAP` where the view's cohorts share units under `overlap: "allow"` (`overlapping` then tells the pack to compute no between-cohort value, §7.4), the view's static caveats and `NOT_ESTIMABLE` where a value other than a suppressed one is; these are the core's whatever the entry's `caveats` list. A pack's analysis has no chart (§8.5): a visual output is a render specification among its values (§10.1). `run` is not called twice to check that it is deterministic (§9.3 asks it of the pack), but its values' digest is held by a golden test of a test pack's view. A pack analysis's segments meet `TextSegment`'s guard as the core's do (D399). | a pack's result must be a result: the envelope checks what it can of a shape it cannot know, and the pack's own schema the rest; a pack's failure refuses its view with a message that quotes nothing of its data, as a leaf compiler's does (D285); what a pack gives is read by the pack's code as much as by the core's, so the guard covers both |
